<meta>This page continues a diplomatic argument regarding the legal rights of the Palatine family and the broader implications for the estates of the Holy Roman Empire. The author is likely an Elector explaining why they cannot attend a personal meeting with the Emperor.</meta>

we also remain steadfast once again; we cannot find within ourselves how the clause attached to **Your <term>Imperial Majesty's</term>** resolution regarding the **<term>Palatine children</term>** and **<term>agnates</term>** <note>male-line relatives who share a common male ancestor</note>—and the suggestion that such matters should be directed toward an amicable settlement or legal process—could be useful for peace and quiet or conclude without further complications. This is because the **<term>rights</term>** <note>original: "jura"</note> of the agnates have been made entirely uncertain and are subjected to **disputes** and the uncertain outcome of the law. For it is undoubtedly true that **<term>acquired rights</term>** <note>original: "jura quęsita"; rights that have been legally established and cannot be easily revoked</note>, especially those gained **<term>by one's own action</term>** <note>original: "per proprium factum"</note>, cannot be taken from anyone; much less should the innocent be punished alongside the guilty.

If we were now to help **<term>deliberate</term>** <note>original: "deliberiren"</note> in our personal presence how, when, where, and in what manner the amicable negotiation or—in view of it—the legal **process** should be initiated, we would not only have to approve all that has preceded it, but also hold the rights of the agnates as uncertain. This would bring upon all Electoral and Princely Houses who hold **<term>collective enfeoffment</term>** <note>original: "gesampte Handt"; a feudal legal arrangement where multiple relatives hold a property or title together</note> such a **<term>prejudicial precedent</term>** <note>original: "præjudicium"</note> that is irreparable and unjustifiable toward the Electoral and Princely Houses. To say nothing of how and in what manner such a **<term>judgment</term>** <note>original: "judicium"</note> should be formed, because, as indicated, all Electoral and Princely Houses are interested in this, and we cannot find agreement within ourselves.

Therefore, **Your <term>Imperial Majesty</term>** may sufficiently perceive from this why and for what reasons we do not consider it appropriate to appear in person under these circumstances, much less deviate from our indicated, well-founded **<term>votes</term>** <note>original: "votis"</note> and **<term>objections</term>** <note>original: "contradictionibus"</note>. We cannot allow ourselves, our **<term>Electoral posterity</term>** <note>original: "Churfr: posteritet"; future generations of the Elector's house</note>, and all other Electoral and Princely Houses to be subjected to such a **prejudicial precedent**, which would be unjustifiable and blameworthy for us. Not to mention the great danger with which our people and lands are now entirely surrounded, and how we are often approached daily by our subjects for urgent rescue, protection, and defense.

<summary>The author argues that the Emperor's proposed legal resolution for the Palatine children's inheritance is flawed because it treats established rights as uncertain, potentially creating a dangerous legal precedent for all noble houses. He also declines a personal meeting with the Emperor, citing both these legal concerns and the immediate military danger threatening his own lands.</summary>

Num. 15.

Thus we most humbly pray **Your <term>Imperial Majesty</term>** <note>original: "E.K.M." or Eure Kaiserliche Majestät</note> to most graciously note, accept, and receive our excuse, and to certainly believe and consider that—since our heavy duty, **<term>Fundamental Law</term>**, established **<term>Constitutions</term>** <note>original: "Constitutiones"</note>, and other ordinances remind us of a different path than what has been shown—we would otherwise demonstrate ourselves toward **Your Imperial Majesty** as duty itself requires. And because the highly necessary and long-desired peace is in **Your Imperial Majesty’s** hands, we do not doubt that **Your Imperial Majesty**, as a **peace-loving Emperor**, will bountifully bestow that same peace upon the **Holy Roman Empire** and its members, clearing away everything that hinders it, and not permit the **Holy Roman Empire** and its members to be brought to total ruin under your Imperial government, but rather be saved therein, etc.

*NB.* <note>Nota Bene: "Note well"</note> Look here, you **<term>Romanists</term>** <note>supporters of the Roman Catholic party or Roman legal influence in the Empire</note>, at a truly German, steadfast spirit. You find no reed blown to and fro by the wind, nor one who wears soft clothing <note>A biblical allusion to John the Baptist, suggesting the Elector is a man of rugged integrity rather than a fickle courtier</note>; you find instead that in understanding and wisdom he is not only your equal, but in highly praiseworthy German deeds—especially **regarding the preservation of the Peace and Liberty of Germany** <note>original: "quò ad conservationem Pacis & Libertatis Germaniæ"</note>—he far, far surpasses you. **Truly** <note>original: "sanè"</note>, just as you here publicly hear his true German, patriotic, and infinitely wise opinion, you will also feel his power, might, and strength in deed and in truth—unless you drop your long-planned **<term>execution</term>** <note>referring to a "Reichsexekution," a legal-military enforcement action</note> against **His <term>Electoral Grace</term>** <note>original: "S. Churf. G."</note> and those of his religion, and leave them in peace. You will see that you have deceived yourselves with the too-easy **judgment** <note>original: "judicio"</note> passed by His Electoral Grace and found in the ***<term>Prodromus</term>*** <note>likely referring to a published legal "fore-runner" or preliminary treatise</note>. He does not carry the sword in vain; the **rue** <note>original: "Rauten." A pun on the rue plant, which is bitter, and the "Rautenkranz" (crancelin) on the Saxon coat of arms</note> is very bitter, but healing: he only needs to catch a scent of it.

<summary>The Elector appeals to the Emperor as a peace-bringer to save the Empire from ruin, while a sharp editorial note warns Catholic "Romanists" that the Elector of Saxony is a steadfast patriot who will defend German liberties by the sword if necessary.</summary>

Num. 15.

Thus we most humbly pray **Your Imperial Majesty** <note>original: "E. K. M." or Eure Kaiserliche Majestät</note> to most graciously note, accept, and receive our excuse, and to certainly believe and consider that—since our heavy duty, **<term>Fundamental Law</term>** <note>The basic laws governing the structure of the Holy Roman Empire, such as the Golden Bull</note>, established **<term>Constitutions</term>** <note>original: "Constitutiones"</note>, and other ordinances do not remind or direct us otherwise—we would otherwise demonstrate ourselves toward **Your Imperial Majesty** as duty itself requires. And because the highly necessary and long-desired peace is in **Your Imperial Majesty’s** hands, we do not doubt that **Your Imperial Majesty**, as a **peace-loving Emperor**, will bountifully bestow that same peace upon the **Holy Roman Empire** and its members, clearing away everything that hinders it, and not permit the **Holy Roman Empire** and its members to be brought to total ruin under your Imperial government, but rather be rescued therein, etc.

*NB.* <note>Nota Bene: "Note well"</note> Look here, you **<term>Romanists</term>** <note>A derogatory term for supporters of the Pope or those favoring Roman legal influence over traditional German liberties</note>, at a truly German, unshakeable spirit. You find no reed blown to and fro by the wind, nor one who wears soft clothing <note>A biblical allusion to Matthew 11:7-8; the author suggests the Elector is a man of rugged integrity rather than a fickle courtier</note>; you find instead that in understanding and wisdom he is not only your equal, but in highly praiseworthy German deeds—especially **regarding the preservation of the Peace and Liberty of Germany** <note>original Latin: "quò ad conservationem Pacis & Libertatis Germaniæ"</note>—he far, far surpasses you. **Truly** <note>original Latin: "sanè"</note>, just as you here publicly hear his true German, patriotic, infinite, and highly wise opinion: so shall you feel his power, force, and strength in work and deed (if you do not drop your long-held **<term>execution</term>** <note>A legal or military enforcement of an Imperial decree</note> against **His Electoral Grace** <note>original: "S. Churf. G." or Seine Kurfürstliche Gnaden</note> and his fellow believers, and leave them in peace). You will find that you have deceived yourselves (with the all-too-easy **judgment** <note>original Latin: "judicio"</note> made by His Electoral Grace and found in the ***Prodromus*** <note>Latin for "precursor"; likely a reference to a specific published defense or introductory text</note>). He does not bear the sword in vain <note>A biblical reference to the authority of a ruler to punish, from Romans 13:4</note>; likewise, the **rue** <note>original: "Rauten." The rue is a bitter herb found on the coat of arms of the Elector of Saxony; the author implies the Elector’s power is a "bitter medicine"</note> is very sharp, but healing: he needs only to give you a whiff of it <note>original: "er braucht nur ein Nasen," an idiom suggesting a sharp rebuke or a "nose full" of something unpleasant</note>.

Num. 15.

## The Treaty of Mainz / A Most Manifest
### Violation. <note>original: "Contraventio manifestissima"</note>

When the treaty established at Mainz <note>The "Mentzischer Vertrag" refers to the Peace of Prague (1635) or related regional agreements signed in Mainz during the Thirty Years' War</note> was first put into effect, all the **United Estates** <note>The "Unirte Stände" refers to the Protestant alliance of princes and cities within the Holy Roman Empire</note> drew a certain comfort from it; indeed, they let no doubt enter their minds that anything would be done contrary to it. However, the reality has proven to be quite different—in fact, the exact opposite has occurred (as the previously recounted violations sufficiently prove). Furthermore, even more can be concluded from what follows:

For several Estates of the Empire, at the recently held **Assembly of Electors and Princes** <note>original: "Chur- und Fürstentag"</note> in Regensburg, complained most loudly about the failure to uphold the treaty. In doing so, they most humbly prayed that **His Imperial Majesty** <note>original: "J. K. M." (Ihre Kaiserliche Majestät)</note> would issue the most gracious orders and ensure that the Treaty of Mainz, in all its clauses, be held firm and fast. To this, they were given the following answer: That their request seemed "strange" to the court, thereby implying that they did not rightly understand the treaty themselves. It was suggested that **His Imperial Majesty** should therefore appoint several deputies to provide an **interpretation** <note>original: "Interpretatio"</note> of the treaty for them. 

This is what it has now come to with the Free German Estates of the Empire: that they are no longer supposed to be able to understand the very treaties and negotiations they themselves established. But they can easily imagine and picture for themselves what kind of "interpretation" that is likely to be.

When I look around at all the **United Evangelical Electors and Estates** and see how they have been ruined by the Roman Catholic forces, I cannot find that the Treaty of Mainz has been upheld in any way. The **Duke of Württemberg**, who had less than nothing to do with this unrest, has been forced to maintain several thousand soldiers for **defense** <note>original: "defension"</note> due to daily hostile incursions against his Duchy—which occurred many and frequent times with a thousand horsemen or even more—and yet **His Princely Grace** <note>original: "Seiner F. G." (Seiner Fürstlichen Gnaden)</note> has still [lost] several villages,

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

as Oberacker and Helbrun—which they also attempted many times against the capable town of Unteröweißheim, though in vain due to the strong resistance—were plundered, set on fire, and the country folk and soldiers, whomever they encountered, were cut down. The captain was captured and held for ransom <note>original: "ransionirt"</note>, and they behaved worse than the Turks. Even the innocent fief-people of **His Princely Grace** <note>original: "J. F. G." (Ihre Fürstliche Gnaden)</note> were not spared; some of their subjects were ruined to the very marrow and bone, and some villages, such as Flehingen, were burned. Thus it has fared for those of Ansbach, Kulmbach, Palatinate-Zweibrücken, Hesse-Kassel, Margrave-Baden, and all those Protestants who hoped to enjoy the benefits of the Treaty of Mainz <note>original: "Meutzischen Vertrags"</note>. The Imperial Cities <term>Reichsstätte (Free cities that held their authority directly from the Emperor rather than a local prince)</term>, for their part, have experienced (as mentioned before) that they were certainly not well served by it. This is what it means to have "trusted the fox" when he solemnly declares <note>original: "Sincerirt"</note> that he no longer wishes to eat chickens. One must still expect such further proceedings, especially the Imperial Cities; this is even more strongly suspected when one considers what they now have to expect from the lowly Counts who possess scarcely a handful of land and people. 

Count Fritz von Solms—who for many years received much grace and many favors from the **Electoral Palatinate** <note>original: "Chur-Pfalz"</note>—recently requested of Duke Johann Friedrich of Württemberg to be allowed to recruit a regiment of foot and a regiment of horse within his Duchy. He claimed <note>original: "Vorwendung"</note> that he intended to use them against the Turks, adding that he would lead them away immediately and wished to muster them upon the subjects of Ulm. 

Things have now come to such a pass that one Christian, for the sake of Spanish money <note>referring to subsidies from the Spanish Habsburgs to Catholic forces</note>, calls another a "Turk" <note>At this time, "Turk" was a common rhetorical shorthand for an infidel or a person of extreme cruelty</note>. Even a mere Count does not shrink from making such dangerous and far-reaching demands of such a prominent Prince of the Empire. It is greatly to be wondered at that now everyone who is simply able to do so presumes to move against the Imperial Cities; since they have already weakened <note>original: "enervirt"</note> the lesser ones, they do not shrink from moving against the more powerful ones as well. For they have already been instructed on how they should proceed against them:

Num. 15.

## The Treaty of Mainz / *Violation*
->*most manifest.*<-
<note>original: "Mentzischen Vertrags / Contraventio manifestissima."</note>

As the Treaty established at Mainz proceeded, all the **United Estates** <note>The "Vnirte Stände" refers to the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant states within the Holy Roman Empire</note> drew a certain comfort, indeed letting no doubt enter their minds that anything would be done contrary to it. However, it has turned out quite differently, and in fact, the complete opposite has been found (as the previously mentioned **violations** <note>original: "contraventiones"</note> sufficiently prove). Even more can be concluded from the following: 

Several Estates of the Empire, at the most recently held **Assembly of Electors and Princes** <note>original: "Chur- vnd Fürstentag"; a high-level diplomatic meeting of the Empire's most powerful rulers</note> in Regensburg, complained most loudly about the failure to uphold the treaty. Therein, they most humbly prayed that **His Imperial Majesty** <note>Emperor Ferdinand II</note> would make the most gracious arrangements and ensure that the Treaty of Mainz, in all its clauses, be firmly and steadfastly maintained. To them, the following answer was given: That their request seemed strange, implying that they did not rightly understand the treaty; therefore, several deputies should be appointed by His Imperial Majesty to **interpret** <note>original: "interpretiren"</note> the treaty for them. 

This is what it has come to for the **Free Germans of the Empire** <note>The "Reichs-Freyen Teutschen" refers to those states and cities that were "Imperial Immediate," meaning they answered directly to the Emperor without an intermediate lord</note>: that they are supposedly no longer able to understand the treaties and transactions they themselves established. But what kind of **interpretation** <note>original: "Interpretatio"</note> that will be, they can easily **imagine** <note>original: "imaginiren"</note> and picture for themselves.

When I look around at all the United Evangelical Electors, Princes, and Estates and see how they have been ruined by the Roman Catholic people, I cannot find that the Treaty of Mainz has been upheld in any respect. The Duke of **Württemberg**, who has had less than nothing to do with this unrest, has been forced to maintain several thousand soldiers for **defense** <note>original: "defension"</note> due to daily hostile incursions against his Duchy—which have occurred many times with a thousand horsemen or more—and yet **His Princely Grace** <note>original: "Seiner F. G." or Seiner Fürstlichen Gnaden</note> has had several villages...

<meta>This page continues the warnings to the Protestant Estates of the Holy Roman Empire. The previous page discussed how the Imperial Court began "interpreting" treaties in ways that stripped Free Cities of their rights. This page moves into the specific collapse of the Protestant Union and the tactical mistakes made by Lutheran leaders who believed they would be spared if they abandoned their Calvinist allies.</meta>

O you honorable Evangelical Free and Imperial Cities, **beware** <note>original: "cavete vobis"</note>, otherwise you will lament **too late** <note>original: "post festum," literally "after the feast"</note> along with Dr. Hoe <note>Matthias Hoe von Hoënegg (1580–1645) was a powerful Lutheran court preacher in Saxony who famously distrusted Calvinists more than Catholics, often advising the Elector of Saxony to side with the Emperor.</note> (since he did not believe, regarding the Calvinists, that after their suppression, it would also be the Lutherans' turn). You will lament and say: we did not think that the unlearned <note>The original "Idioten" refers to commoners or those without specialized theological/political training.</note> could have had prophecies about these matters. Whether this Count did this **by his own authority, or rather by the instinct or command of a third party** <note>original: "propriâ authoritate, vel potius ex instinctu aut mandato Tertij"</note> is unknown to me; every man’s conscience is his best witness. But that it did not happen, or was rejected, I can imagine the reasons for myself, which are indeed not entirely hidden from me.

### ->Number 16.<-
## ->Regarding the Treaty of Bingen and its<-
### ->Violations.<- <note>original: "Contraventionen"</note>

What great joy the dissolution of the Union <note>The Protestant Union (1608–1621) was a coalition of Protestant states. Its dissolution was a major victory for the Catholic League.</note> brought to the Papists <note>A common, though derogatory, period term for Roman Catholics.</note> is openly attested by the German Commander at Heilbronn, the Lord of Wolckenstein (who, in this Palatine misfortune, proved himself very "knightly" in both words and deeds). For during a meal, he spoke these **formal words** <note>original: "formalia"</note> without shame: "Praise God, we have good news; the Union is torn apart, which we have long desired; now we have won, and can conquer what we have long craved and sought." Note here how the Union was a thorn in their eyes, and how its death became their life and resurrection.

Some have argued that the treaty established at Bingen <note>The Treaty of Bingen (1621) saw the Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel forced to withdraw from the Protestant Union under military pressure from the Spanish General Spinola.</note> between Landgrave Moritz of Kassel and Spinola gave the United Protestants a path toward a similar **agreement** <note>original: "accordinen"</note>. For because he—as such a distinguished Prince who shared the same religion and faith as the Electoral Palatinate—was nonetheless the first to withdraw from the Union and **come to terms** <note>original: "accordiret"</note> with the enemy, agreeing that he would assist the Electoral Palatinate neither **directly nor indirectly** <note>original: "directè noch indirectè assistiren"</note>, then the others, and especially the Imperial...

[Imperial] Cities caused; for they were said to have feared that the **Electoral Palatinate** <note>The territories ruled by the Count Palatine, a leading Protestant prince.</note>, as the head of the **Union** <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608 to defend their interests against the Catholic League.</note>, would not be properly defended. As for what kind of defense they then had to hope for: I leave this to be judged for what it is worth and report here with the utmost truth that the highly esteemed **Landgrave** <note>Likely Landgrave Moritz of Hesse-Kassel (r. 1592–1627).</note> never allowed such a thing to enter his mind or his dreams. For as soon as **His Princely Grace** <note>original: "Seine F. G." (Seine Fürstliche Gnaden)</note> reported to Heidelberg how he had been shamefully betrayed by his **councilors** <note>original: "Rähte"</note>—that they had negotiated and concluded more than they were authorized to do in their **mandate** <note>original: "mandato"</note>—it was entirely against his will. He had also punished them for it as much as time and opportunity allowed. Therefore, one should never believe that he had approved or sanctioned such a thing; he has completely **retracted** <note>original: "retractirt"</note> and **revoked** <note>original: "revocirt"</note> it, and such a thing should never be suspected or feared from him. For my part, I would not have believed that such wicked councilors could have been found in **Hesse**.

But what has this treaty—I will not speak of the sovereign prince, but of the **territorial estates** <note>original: "Landſchafft"; the representative bodies of the nobility, clergy, and towns.</note> upon which these councilors relied—helped, benefited, or protected them from danger? Less than nothing. Since **Spinola** <note>Ambrogio Spinola (1569–1630), a famous Spanish general who commanded the Army of Flanders during the Palatinate campaign.</note>, while negotiations were effectively still ongoing (just as happened with the **Treaty of Lindau** <note>A 1622 treaty that saw the Graubünden regions forced into a disadvantageous peace with the Habsburgs.</note> regarding the **people of Graubünden** <note>original: "Bündnern"</note>), quickly launched an attack on the city of **St. Goar** <note>original: "Sanct Goaria"</note>, intending to capture it so as to proceed even more unhindered along the **Rhine** <note>original: "Rheinstrom"</note>. Have the **Hessian Estates** <note>original: "Heſſiſche Landſtände"</note> not since then experienced in deed what kind of fruits their **Accord of Bingen** <note>A 1621 agreement in which Hesse-Kassel attempted to buy peace through neutrality and financial contributions.</note> has borne and brought them? Are they not themselves, along with their subjects, ruined to the very marrow and bone? Has the pious prince not lost **Marburg** <note>original: "Marpurg"</note> because of it, and forfeited the **Abbey of Hersfeld** <note>original: "Abtey Hirſchfeld"</note>, and otherwise suffered such damage and loss that will hardly be overcome?

From this and other frequently violated treaties, we can clearly perceive that all our accommodating, according, and contracting serves no other purpose than to give the **Roman Catholics** <note>original: "Röm: Catholiſchen"</note> good opportunities and occasions...

Therefore, no matter how glistening, beautiful, high, and dear all agreements <note>original: "Accord"</note> are promised, I disapprove <note>original: "improbire"</note> of entering into any more of them. On the contrary, I purely decree <note>original: "Purè Statuire"</note> that we should seize and take up the means permitted and allowed by God, Law, and Nature.

### Num. 17.
## Extract,
# Of a short Information published in print,
->concerning the affairs of the Electoral Palatinate <note>The territories ruled by the Elector Palatine, a central figure in the outbreak of the Thirty Years' War.</note>, in which the peace-seeking<-
->actions of the King of England and His Majesty’s son-in-law <note>Frederick V, Elector Palatine, who married Elizabeth Stuart, daughter of King James I of England.</note> are to be seen as clear as day,<-
->like in a mirror; and conversely, the violations <note>original: "Contraventiones"</note> that<-
->occurred against the high promises and treaties of the Emperor,<-
->the Spaniard, and the Infanta <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note>,<-
->are clearly visible.<-

In the first main point, it was shown how, right at the beginning of the Bohemian unrest <note>original: "Böhmisches Unwesen," referring to the Bohemian Revolt (1618), which triggered the Thirty Years' War.</note>, the Elector Palatine (out of a special desire he had to see peace restored in that kingdom and the subjects reconciled with their King) sent an envoy to His Imperial Majesty in Vienna. He had him humbly request that the Emperor master himself, cease the violence of arms, and extinguish the spark of this war while it was still in the ashes. But it was in vain; for after the envoy had persisted for many months in succession, he achieved nothing further than causing great expense.

After the death of Emperor Matthias <note>Matthias died in 1619, leading to a succession crisis.</note>, the King of England—as a testament to his well-meaning affection toward the common welfare of the German lands—sent the Viscount of Doncaster <note>James Hay, 1st Earl of Carlisle, a key diplomat for James I.</note>, his Privy Councilor and Chamberlain, as an ambassador to Archduke Ferdinand, the then-appointed King of

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Elector Palatine’s diplomatic attempts to mediate the Bohemian Revolt. It describes the diplomatic snubbing of King James I’s ambassador during the Imperial Election of 1619 and the beginning of Spanish military mobilization in 1620.</meta>

Bohemia and to other Princes of the Empire, he dispatched an ambassador to mediate a treaty between the said Archduke <note>Ferdinand II, then Archduke of Austria and King-elect of Bohemia.</note> and the Bohemian Estates <note>The assembly of the various ranks of nobility and representatives in Bohemia.</note>. Through this path of amicability, he hoped to bring both parties to rest and to prevent the evil that has since miserably oppressed—is currently oppressing, and will continue to oppress—not only the Roman Empire but all of Christendom, unless God changes it. The said ambassador then met His Highness Ferdinand at Regensburg, just as he was on his way to travel to Frankfurt for the Imperial Election <note>The ceremony where the seven Prince-electors chose the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>. He received an answer from him that nothing could be undertaken there regarding the Bohemian affairs; therefore, he should travel back with him to Frankfurt, where something could be more conveniently concluded with the advice of the Electors.

When the ambassador hoped to be admitted to Frankfurt for this purpose, he was nevertheless refused under the pretext that, by virtue of the Golden Bull <note>The fundamental law of the Holy Roman Empire, which forbade foreign representatives from being present in the city during the election of a new Emperor to prevent outside interference.</note>, no foreign lords or ambassadors were permitted in the city during an ongoing election. Consequently, he went to Hanau and had to wait there for a considerable time at great expense (as he had many people with him). And although he very often and urgently requested a hearing, he was unable to obtain it until, in the meantime, Ferdinand was led into the Council of Electors and subsequently elected Emperor. Since the Emperor—having achieved his intended goal through his admission into the number of Electors as a King of Bohemia—left the Bohemian matter to be resolved by the Electors, the intended mediation <note>original: "verzichtung"</note> of the mentioned ambassador turned out to be a complete and hollow failure, and he was sent back to where he came from. This is the first mockery <note>original: "Spott"</note> that His Royal Majesty of Great Britain experienced during his negotiations.

In the year 1620, when the King of Spain and Archduke Albert <note>Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note> raised a mighty military armament in the Burgundian Provinces <note>The Spanish-controlled territories in the Low Countries.</note> in the months of July and August, and His Royal Majesty in Great Britain was reported from other quarters that such preparations were against the Lower <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "Pfalz" indicates the next word is "Palatinate."</note>

Considering the threat to the **Palatinate**, they sent their ambassadors, **Conway** and **Weston**, to the aforementioned <term>Archduke</term> <note>Archduke Albert VII, the Governor of the Spanish Netherlands based in Brussels.</note> in Brussels, to warn him against this hostile invasion of the Palatinate. They offered to apply all possible diligence to mediate and help bring everything to a peaceful agreement. The response given to them was that the King of Spain and His Serene Highness <note>original: "J. D." for Ihre Durchlaucht, referring to the Archduke.</note> had assembled and equipped an army for the Emperor's benefit, but nothing had yet been decided as to where it should actually be used. It should be noted here, however, that while this scornful answer was given to the ambassadors, these troops were already in full march toward the Palatinate.

Immediately after the <term>Declaration of the Imperial Ban</term> <note>The "Reichsacht," a legal decree by the Emperor that declared Frederick V an outlaw, stripped him of his titles, and permitted the seizure of his lands.</note> against the Count Palatine, the **United Princes and Estates** <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note> sent their ambassadors to the Emperor to seek a stay of the execution of the Ban. They asked that His <term>Majesty</term> <note>original: "Mt:" for Majestät (Ferdinand II).</note> allow for an amicable resolution to prevent the many distressing hardships that would inevitably follow. They promised to guide and persuade the said Count Palatine to accept everything required to preserve the common good and peace, provided it was reasonable. But they were answered in the Emperor's name that they could expect nothing other than fire and sword.

The **Danish** ambassadors, sent by their King for the same purpose, were paid and dismissed with the same coin.

And because the <term>Elector Palatine</term> <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King."</note> had entrusted and completely surrendered this entire matter to the **Royal Majesties in England and Denmark**, offering to accept whatever they might negotiate and settle in his name (provided it did not go against honor and conscience), His Royal Majesty in England took the lead. The Count Palatine specifically left it to him to handle and undertake whatever he deemed necessary for the recovery of his land and status, and for the restoration of a lasting Imperial peace. <meta>This page continues the narrative of diplomatic efforts by the King of Great Britain to mediate the conflict following the Imperial Ban against Frederick V, the Count Palatine.</meta>

willingly took this mediation upon himself and—as he has done repeatedly until now—promised to apply all possible diligence to it. He even vowed, in case of necessity, to risk everything God has granted him in this world for it; yet he always maintained that it was best and most advisable to use peaceful means before they would have to resort to serious action.

This prompted His Royal Majesty of Great Britain to send **Baron Digby** <note>John Digby, 1st Earl of Bristol, a key diplomat for King James I.</note>, his Privy Councilor and Chamberlain, in the month of June to His Imperial Majesty <note>Emperor Ferdinand II</note>, with the command and authority to bring matters to a successful conclusion and settlement. **Archduke Albert** <note>Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note> had just granted a <term>truce</term> <note>original: "Anstand," a temporary cessation of hostilities.</note> for several weeks to the **Lower Palatinate**, which was now coming to an end. The **Upper Palatinate** was still under the control of the <term>Count Palatine</term> <note>Frederick V, whose lands were being targeted for execution of the Ban.</note>, though not without fear due to the massive war preparations that the **Duke of Bavaria** <note>Maximilian I, who had been promised Frederick's lands and electoral title by the Emperor.</note> was making at that time to seize it under the name of an Imperial commission entrusted to him. The ambassador **Digby** applied all possible diligence and propriety to carry out his assigned duties, making increasingly forceful and earnest appeals to His Imperial Majesty from day to day. The Emperor then let it be known that he desired nothing more than peace, provided that the Count Palatine would first humble himself and make sufficient restitution to His Majesty. To this, they constantly added one condition or another, merely for the purpose (as the outcome has proven) of gaining time. They did this so that once the truce had expired, the Duke of Bavaria could have a more convenient opportunity to seize the Upper Palatinate, unhindered by **Mansfeld** <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a mercenary general fighting for the Protestant cause who had troops positioned near the border.</note>, who had gathered some troops on the **Bohemian borders**. When the aforementioned truce came to an end, the Spanish restarted the war in the Lower Palatinate anew, without His Imperial Majesty being willing to agree to any suspension or postponement of it, citing various newly sought, pretextual reasons. <meta>This page continues the narrative of diplomatic frustration for King James I of Great Britain as he attempts to mediate a peace between the Emperor and his son-in-law, Frederick V. The contradictory nature of these letters—issued at the same time and concerning the same matter—is clearly shown in the answer given to the Ambassador on November 8, 1621, by the **Infanta** <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, daughter of Philip II of Spain and Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note>. All of this was done so that they would not lose the advantage they believed they now held in their hands through the occupation of the **Upper Palatinate** and a large part of the **Lower Palatinate** <note>The Palatinate was divided into two geographically separate territories: the Upper (near Bavaria) and the Lower (along the Rhine).</note>. Indeed, nothing remained to be captured except **Heidelberg**, **Mannheim**, and **Frankenthal**, along with a few other minor places. To exploit this advantage, the **Duke of Bavaria** <note>Maximilian I, leader of the Catholic League.</note> ordered the greater part of the **League Army** <term>Bundtheer</term> <note>The combined forces of the Catholic League.</note> to march downward. Under various invented pretexts, this army joined with the **Spanish** forces. Consequently, whatever the **King of Great Britain** <note>James I</note> requested in Brussels to allow the poor Palatinate a brief moment to catch its breath and to mediate a peace was entirely in vain. The same was true for the request made in **Vienna**, where they pretended with great outward show to be inclined to comply with His Royal Majesty’s wishes. The King desired nothing more than the full restoration of his **son-in-law's** <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King."</note> lands—both the Upper and Lower Palatinate—and his **Electoral dignity** <term>Churfürstliche Würden</term> <note>The prestigious right to vote in the election of the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>. In return, Frederick offered on his part to renounce the **Crown of Bohemia** and, regarding the "humiliation" <note>An official act of submission or apology to the Emperor.</note> that the other side pressed for so strongly (though without any real intention of accepting it), he offered to do everything that could be done without violating the **Imperial statutes** <term>Reichssatzungen</term>.

Meanwhile, the **League Army** and the **Spanish** continued to ravage and ruin the **Lower Palatinate** out of necessity, so that the **Elector** <note>Frederick V</note> was forced to go there in person and, with the loyal assistance of **Mansfeld's** <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a prominent mercenary general fighting for the Protestant cause.</note> troops, offer all possible resistance. The **King of Great Britain**, seeing that the enemy grew stronger every day and was intent on seizing the Lower Palatinate as well as the Upper, saw fit to write to the **Emperor** <note>Ferdinand II</note> via a special courier on November 12, 1621. In the name of his son-in-law, he offered peace terms which, in his opinion, the Emperor—who was being portrayed everywhere as desiring nothing but peace—would find virtually <note>The text ends mid-word with "gleich-", continued by the catchword "sam" to form "gleichsam" (virtually/as it were).</note>

Frederick V is forced to take up arms again alongside General Mansfeld to defend his remaining Rhine strongholds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of diplomatic deception surrounding the Palatinate. It details how the English Ambassador was led to believe a truce was possible while the Emperor secretly ordered continued military aggression.</meta>

[The Emperor] gave the aforementioned Ambassador hope with smooth words and good promises, namely that the Duke of Bavaria (whose commission, by the Emperor's command, had been suspended upon the previous request of the often-mentioned Ambassador) had not received any single new command or commission to execute the <term>Ban</term> <note>original: "Aacht"; the Imperial Ban, a legal decree stripping a ruler of his lands and rights.</note> against the <term>Upper Palatinate</term> <note>original: "Oberpfalz"; the northern territory of the Elector Palatine, bordering Bavaria.</note>, nor would he receive such a command in the future. Finally, His Imperial Majesty <note>original: "jhr K. Mt." for Kaiserliche Majestät, referring to Ferdinand II.</note> also granted—upon the repeated and urgent plea of the Ambassador and certain promises and conditions offered by him—a peace negotiation and meeting. He also wrote to the Spanish Infanta <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note> on September 11, granting her full power to conclude with His Royal Majesty of Great Britain (should the circumstances there permit) a <term>truce</term> <note>original: "Anstand"; a temporary suspension of hostilities.</note> or even a total cessation of arms in the <term>Lower Palatinate</term> <note>original: "vnder Pfalz"; the region around Heidelberg and the Rhine.</note>, in the hope that through such means they might reach a friendly treaty for a complete peace and the settling of all disputes.

The Ambassador, relying on such manifold declarations, promises, and good words, and trusting that nothing further could be obtained, took his leave from the Emperor and hurried back, intending to bring the matter to a favorable conclusion by virtue of the commission given to the Infanta. But upon his return, he learned and saw for himself that the Duke of Bavaria had already subjected the entire <term>Upper Palatinate</term> to himself and was forcing the subjects there to swear him homage. He complained most vehemently about this to the Emperor in a separate letter dated October 5, stating that all of this was directly contrary to the Imperial declaration and the speeches, presentations, and assurances provided by the Imperial Court Council. 

When he arrived in Brussels, they employed every imaginable means to obtain a <term>truce</term>, but they were unable to achieve it in the slightest. This was because the Emperor, behind the back of the Infanta’s Ambassador, had written another letter in which he commanded her exactly the opposite: namely, that she should enter into no <term>truce</term> at all, but rather that His Imperial Majesty intended to order Don Corduba <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba, a Spanish military commander.</note> to continue the offensive.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the diplomatic maneuvering between King James I of England and Emperor Ferdinand II. ...it would be impossible to refuse. To place the matter and his offer on an even more secure footing, he exhorted the **Elector Palatine**, his **son-in-law** <note>Frederick V, who married James I's daughter Elizabeth Stuart.</note>, to promise and approve all of this through a public, binding **instrument** <note>A formal legal document.</note>, signed by his own hand and validated with his **seal**. This he then did and issued, **dated November 25**, in which he certainly prioritized the common tranquility and the **peace of the Holy Roman Empire** over his private concerns and all the specific needs of himself and his House.

**Notwithstanding** that at the **Courts of Vienna** and **Brussels**, the **English ambassadors** and **servants** were filled with nothing but the loudly proclaimed **Imperial inclination toward peace** and the **resolution** which the Emperor had finally reached to comply with **His Royal Majesty** <note>King James I of Great Britain.</note>, he nevertheless by no means accepted the aforementioned proposed **peace terms**, but flatly rejected them. He thus also released the **Elector Palatine** from his voluntary **obligation** and **written agreement**, as if declaring it void.

**Thereupon**, he sent the **Count of Schwarzenburg** to **England** with a sack full of nothing but empty words of honor and foreign **courtesies** <note>original: "welschen Courteisien," a phrase implying insincere or overly formal Mediterranean etiquette.</note>. He gave the **Infanta** <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note> new orders to hold discussions with the **King of England** regarding a **truce** <term>truce</term> <note>original: "Anstand," a temporary suspension of hostilities.</note> or a total **cessation of arms**, or even a **peace treaty**. Yet, in the meantime, he allowed the war in the **Lower Palatinate** to continue unabated. He withheld the aforementioned offered **peace terms** from the **Protestant Imperial Princes** and **Estates**, but he communicated them to **Bethlen Gabor** <note>The Prince of Transylvania, an ally of the Protestant cause.</note> for the purpose of luring him into a **peace** (which he then accepted). The result was that immediately thereafter, the largest part of the Imperial army—stationed in **Austria, Bohemia**, and elsewhere—was suddenly poured like a **flood** over the **Lower Palatinate** to deal it the final blow.

**Nonetheless**, they continued to assure **His Royal Majesty of Great Britain** of **restitution**; they continually offered **peace**...

<meta>This page continues the account of King James I’s diplomatic efforts to secure peace in the Palatinate. The previous page ended with the failure of negotiations in Vienna and the continued military pressure from the Catholic League and Spanish forces.</meta>

...conduct; to put it briefly, they heaped promises upon promises. And so **His Royal Majesty** <note>James I of England.</note> was moved to further negotiations and sent **Mr. Weston** <note>Sir Richard Weston, a diplomat and future Chancellor of the Exchequer.</note> as ambassador to **Brussels** to treat with the **Infanta** <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note>. She, for her part, was not slow to appoint **Commissioners** <term>Commissarios</term> for this purpose. However, merely to buy time, they demanded a new **mandate** <term>Gewalt</term> <note>A legal power of attorney or formal authorization to negotiate.</note> signed and sealed by the **Count Palatine** <note>Frederick V, James I's son-in-law.</note>. They claimed this was so they could be certain that he would uphold and validate everything negotiated between them. And although His Royal Majesty already possessed sufficient authority based on various letters written by his **son-in-law**, they nevertheless wanted to see a complete and formal mandate. In this, His Royal Majesty willingly yielded to them. He had a full mandate drawn up for his aforementioned ambassador, in both his own name and that of his son-in-law. The son-in-law then signed and confirmed this alongside His Royal Majesty, and it was sent to Brussels. 

However, when it was presented there, the said commissioners found fault with only a single title: **Elector** <term>Churfürst</term> <note>The "Kurfürst" or Prince-Elector was one of the elite group of princes entitled to elect the Holy Roman Emperor. Frederick's removal from this office was a central point of the conflict.</note>. This title belonged to the **Count Palatine** <note>original: "Pfaltzgraven"</note> by birth and inheritance and could never be forfeited. Nevertheless, they asked that the document be changed and renewed to omit this title, claiming they could not in good conscience enter into negotiations based on such a title. Therefore, His Royal Majesty (to remove and lift all their scruples) had the often-mentioned mandate recast in a new form, leaving the title out entirely, though without any detriment or prejudice to the claim itself. This back-and-forth, practiced by the opposing side, was intended solely to **delay the matter indefinitely** <note>original: "vff die lange Banck zuspielen," literally "to play it onto the long bench."</note>. They wanted to prevent His Royal Majesty from sending more than the already promised aid of 8,000 infantry and 1,600 **cavalry** <term>Reißigen</term> <note>Literally "the mounted ones," referring to heavy cavalry or armed riders.</note> into the **Palatinate**, Flanders, or elsewhere, which might have caused a stir and created a **diversion** <note>A military maneuver intended to draw the enemy's attention away from the main objective.</note>.

Now that the said mandate had finally been settled in this manner,

<meta>This page continues the narrative of King James I’s failing diplomatic efforts. Following the rejection of his son-in-law's title in Brussels, the King now faces a strategic betrayal by the Emperor and the brutal military fall of the Palatinate's key cities.</meta>

and when he was sent there again for the second time, **His Royal Majesty** <note>King James I of England.</note> received letters dated June 18th, through which he was informed that peace could not truly be concluded in **Brussels**. This was because the matter concerned the entire **Holy Roman Empire**, and for that reason, he, the **Emperor** <note>Ferdinand II.</note>, had summoned the Imperial **Electors** <term>Chur-Fürsten</term> and several other Princes to meet in **Regensburg**. 

They left it to His Royal Majesty’s discretion whether he wished to appoint and send someone from his side to that location. This contrary and unseemly process appeared very strange to His Royal Majesty; he took it poorly and complained of it in the highest and sharpest terms in his response. 

Furthermore, because the small remaining portion of the **Palatinate** <note>The hereditary lands of James's son-in-law, Frederick V.</note> was in the extreme danger of being lost—it being impossible to resist so many and such strong enemies face-to-face—he, in order to somewhat break the force of this mighty blow, took the cities of **Heidelberg**, **Mannheim**, and **Frankenthal** under his own protection and shield. 

But even this could not prevent the first two from being besieged and bombarded with great fury. **Heidelberg** was taken by storm and force, and was miserably devastated with fire, murder, and plundering; **Mannheim**, however, was forced to surrender by **agreement** <term>Accord</term> <note>A formal surrender treaty usually granting the garrison safe passage.</note>.

Thus, only the single city of **Frankenthal** remained. To preserve it—since His Royal Majesty saw that it would be spared no more under his protection than the other two had been—he **sequestered** <term>sequestrirt</term> it and surrendered it into the hands of the **Infanta** <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, Governor of the Spanish Netherlands.</note> upon her insistence and strong promises. She then entrusted the **government** <term>Gubernament</term> to a Spanish gentleman, **Don Verdugo** <note>Guillermo Verdugo, a military commander serving the Spanish crown.</note>. The **garrison** <term>Guarnison</term> stationed there, due to a lack of pay, daily threatens to plunder the poor citizenry, even though the citizens are not obligated to provide for them.

After this total seizure and devastation of the **Electoral Palatinate** <term>Chur Pfaltz</term>, the Emperor allowed the aforementioned **Diet of Regensburg** to proceed. He attended in person and publicly **invested** <note>A formal ceremony of granting land and titles.</note> the **Duke of Bavaria** <note>Maximilian I, a leader of the Catholic League.</note> with the Palatine Electorate, [dispossessing] the **Count Palatine’s** children, his brother, and all his closest blood relatives—

<meta>This page continues the account of the diplomatic fallout following the loss of the Palatinate. It details a letter from Emperor Ferdinand II to King James I, justifying the transfer of the electoral title to the Duke of Bavaria while offering a hollow legal process for the dispossessed Palatine heirs.</meta>

unlocked / all contrary to the advice and protests given by the **Electors of Saxony** and **Brandenburg** <note>The two primary Protestant Electors of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, both of whom, as Electors of the Empire, pressed for the restoration of the lands and the **Electorate** <term>Chur</term> <note>The office and dignity of an Imperial Elector.</note> through their ambassadors. This, the **Emperor** <note>Ferdinand II.</note> made known to **His Royal Majesty** in **England** through his own letters, dated **Regensburg**, March 5, 1623. In these, he expressed all love and goodwill toward Him, reporting that this transfer of the Electorate to the **House of Bavaria** occurred with the express reservation that thereby (and out of special **Imperial Grace**) **His Royal Majesty’s** grandchildren and the other blood-relatives should not be barred from negotiating hereafter in one or another Imperial City, such as **Nuremberg**, **Augsburg**, **Ulm**, or **Frankfurt**, regarding the rights and claims that each of them believed they had to the Electorate, Title, and Dignity. And in the event they could not agree among themselves on those matters, the cases would be brought before **His Imperial Majesty** and the entire **College of Electors** <term>Churfürstliches Collegio</term> and legally adjudicated, also expecting a prompt judicial verdict thereon, provided they did not otherwise perhaps stand in their own way.

From which reservation **His Royal Majesty** in **Great Britain** could clearly perceive **His Imperial Majesty’s** good affection and well-meaning intention toward His Person and welfare. And this is the brief contents of the cited Imperial letters.

Now one is left to judge this "beautiful declaration" <note>The author likely uses "schöne Erklärung" sarcastically, given the subsequent betrayal described.</note> for oneself; but it must not be kept secret that the Emperor, in the manner described in his aforementioned letters, assumed he had reserved for the **Count Palatine’s** <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King."</note> children, his brother, and blood-relatives the permission to exercise their existing claims and rights against one another legally—yet upon the departure from **Regensburg**, after the **Duke of Bavaria** <note>Maximilian I.</note> [had been granted the title], he confirmed and handed over the **expectancy** <term>Anwartung</term> <note>A legal right to a future succession or title.</note> of the Electorate to **Duke Wolfgang Wilhelm of Neuburg** with letters and seals.

And just as he, the **Emperor**, at all times professed all goodness and all friendship toward **His Royal Majesty** in **England**,

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the diplomatic maneuvers of the 1620s. It transitions from the Emperor's hollow legal offers to the specific role of the Spanish Crown in the dispossession of the Palatinate and the transfer of its electoral dignity to the Duke of Bavaria.</meta>

He also always willingly listened to their friendly and precious—yet vainly submitted—plea for the restoration of the **Palatinate**. In the same manner, the **King of Spain** acted similarly, and continued this for so long that eventually the poor **Palatinate** was plunged into its current, more than pitiable condition, and the **Duke of Bavaria** <note>Maximilian I, who was granted the electoral title taken from the "Winter King" Frederick V.</note> was placed in full possession of the entire **Upper Palatinate** and the greater part of the **Lower Palatinate**, along with the **Electoral Hat** <term>Churhaube</term> <note>The ceremonial cap symbolizing the rank and authority of a Prince-Elector.</note>. 

For although the **King of Spain** frequently assured **His Royal Majesty** in **England** <note>James I.</note>, both in writing and through his ambassadors, that he would do his utmost to ensure the entire **Electoral Palatinate** <term>ChurPfalz</term> along with all its territories might be *restored* <note>original: "restituirt"</note>, with the express promise that in case of necessity he would stand "as one man" with **His Royal Majesty** and unite his own military power with his: nonetheless, when they requested a written certificate of this through their own ambassadors, he gave the answer that he could not do this. He argued that as a middleman, such an obligation would not only hinder his free hand but also his *discretion* <note>original: "arbitrium"</note> in this matter. Above all, however, he claimed such a thing would offend his cousin the **Emperor** <note>Ferdinand II.</note>, and if he were to mix threatening words and military force with the negotiation, it would be nothing other than an impropriety toward **His Imperial Majesty**. Through which answer it is clearly demonstrated that all these Spanish promises were only deceptive words.

To conclude this report, I must recount one more little trick used by the opposing side, which is this: while the **Infanta** of Spain <note>Archduchess Isabella Clara Eugenia, sovereign of the Spanish Netherlands and daughter of Philip II.</note>, acting under an **Imperial Commission** and attempting to mask her actions with a great desire to establish peace and satisfy the **King of England**, she had certain *Articles* and *Points* proposed for a fifteen-month **truce** <term>Anstandt</term> in the **Lower Palatinate** and generally throughout the whole Empire. This was done to the end that, during this time, one could all the better negotiate a lasting peace with one another; so that, if **His Royal Majesty** had [intended] anything in the [matters] just indicated...

<summary>This page describes how the King of Spain used hollow diplomatic promises and the pretense of mediation to delay English intervention while the Duke of Bavaria consolidated control over the Palatinate. It concludes by introducing a proposed fifteen-month truce brokered by the Infanta Isabella Clara Eugenia.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the diplomatic trickery used against Frederick V, the Elector Palatine. It details how he was coerced into signing a truce that his enemies—Spain and the Holy Roman Empire—never intended to honor, specifically to prevent him from uniting with Protestant military allies.</meta>

...the changed points; they allowed themselves to be satisfied with them and signed them, and also sent them to the **Prince-Elector Palatine** <term>Churfürsten Pfaltzgraffen</term> <note>Frederick V, often called the "Winter King," whose lands were being seized by Imperial and Spanish forces.</note> to sign. After he took a short time to reflect—questioning whether he should consent to it given the deceitful words and clauses contained within—he nonetheless signed it as well, following urgent requests and being given good hope by the opposing side for a settlement and accommodation of the matter. 

But all of this was meant for nothing. For afterward, they nonetheless continued to daily assault the poor **Palatine** subjects with all sorts of cruel acts of violence and distress, burdening and tormenting them against these very **Articles of Truce** <term>Anstandts Articul</term>, which they had forged according to their own fantasies based on all sorts of newly sought-after pretenses. The **Spanish-Austrian** purpose of this hypocritical armistice was this: to bind the **Elector Palatine’s** hands and strip him of all power to seek further help and assistance from foreign potentates. In particular, it was to prevent this: that he should not be allowed to use the forces of **Brunswick** <note>The army of Christian of Brunswick, a Protestant commander.</note> and **Mansfeld** <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a mercenary general fighting for the Palatine cause.</note>, which were then on the move, so that **Bethlen Gabor** <note>The Prince of Transylvania and a formidable opponent of the Habsburgs.</note> would also not be prompted to strengthen himself against the **House of Austria**. 

However, as soon as the military force of **Brunswick** was defeated (through God’s providence) <note>Referring to the Battle of Höchst in 1622, where the Protestant forces suffered a major setback.</note>, and **Spain** and **Austria** saw that their plan had completely succeeded, they themselves began to mock and laugh at this truce. They drove their hostilities ever more vehemently, soon granting the **Bergstrasse** <note>original: "Bergstraß," a highly fertile and strategic region in the Rhine valley.</note>—one of the best **Palatine** lands—to the **Archbishop of Mainz**, thereby entangling him even more closely in their party and its protection against the **Elector Palatine**. Indeed, beyond this, they imposed unbearable, persistent, and ever-increasing taxes and **contributions** <term>Contributionen</term> <note>War taxes or forced levies extracted from a population to support an occupying army.</note> upon the poor subjects, who had been sucked dry to the marrow—not only in the **Electoral Palatinate** but also in the **Principality of Simmern**. It should be noted again here that the mentioned **Articles of Truce** were [issued] in the name of the **Emperor** to several **Imperial Princes** <term>Reichsfürsten</term>...

<summary>This page describes a "hypocritical truce" used by Spanish and Austrian forces to isolate the Elector Palatine from his military allies like Mansfeld and Christian of Brunswick. Once the Protestant armies were defeated, the truce was openly mocked, and Palatine lands were distributed to Catholic allies while the population was subjected to crushing war taxes.</summary>

a whole month before / and before they had decided / it was accepted and confirmed in England / and indeed in another form / partly also with different content / shown only for the purpose of seizing every advantage, to confuse the Imperial Electors and Princes, and to turn them away from the **Elector Palatine** <term>Pfaltzgrafen</term> <note>Frederick V, the Elector Palatine, whose lands were being seized by Imperial and Spanish forces during the early stages of the Thirty Years' War.</note>. Some praise and laud these practices / and those who carry them out / saying they treat us rightly; we know it and let ourselves be deceived. --- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Catholic powers—specifically the Habsburgs and the Catholic League—view the Electorate of the Palatinate as a strategic "iron wall" that must never be returned to Protestant hands. It warns that any peace negotiations are merely a front to keep the Protestant princes and their allies, like the King of England, from taking action.</meta>

...hold back [rebellious] deeds. For the Catholics, however, this **Electorate** <term>Churfürstenthumb</term> <note>The Electorate of the Palatinate was one of the most strategically and politically significant territories in the Holy Roman Empire, as its ruler held one of the seven votes to elect the Emperor.</note> can be, for their part, like an iron wall and the safest sanctuary. But if this Electorate, which is well-supplied with financial resources and war munitions, should be abandoned through our negligence, and thus be retaken by the Lutherans and Calvinists through their godless schemes <note>original: "Practiquen"</note> (which it seems is not far off), then the highest danger and disadvantage might arise from this for all Catholics in all of **Germany** <note>original: "Teutschland"</note>.

On the other hand, if this Electorate remains Catholic and in our power, the neighboring Lutherans or Calvinists can do little harm or achieve much; for from this single Electorate alone, all their insolent beginnings can be suppressed, and the Lutherans and Calvinists, together with their religion, can be all the more easily uprooted and exterminated to the very ground. So much depends on this Electorate being maintained in the Catholic faith and in our power. Therefore, no **restitution** <term>Restitution</term> <note>The legal return of the seized Palatinate lands to the Protestant Frederick V.</note> of the Palatinate is to be advised to the Catholics; but rather, an unbearable burden and servitude should be imposed upon the Count Palatine and all Lutherans and Calvinists, so that they may never again come to their former strength or be able to raise themselves up again, etc.

When the Count Palatine and his Honorable **Father-in-law** <term>Schwäher Vatter</term> <note>King James I of England, whose daughter Elizabeth was married to Frederick V of the Palatinate.</note> consider this, they will indeed find how all suggested and presumed "kindly" dealings are in vain—**indeed** <note>original: "imò"</note>—nothing but pure deceptions. And it is very mocking to all who love their honor and Fatherland, as well as entirely shameful and harmful, that their advisors allow themselves to be fooled like children and attend and wait upon such **VOID** negotiations <note>original: "Tractaten"</note>, from which they can gain little honor (as people who always allow themselves to be deceived).

# Chapter Three.

Containing the repeated recent assurances and promises of security given by the Roman Catholics to the **Protestants** <term>Evangelischen</term> <note>At this time, "Evangelical" referred collectively to the Lutheran and Reformed (Calvinist) churches in the Empire.</note>, despite the obvious **violations occurring at an equal pace** <note>original: "pari passu ambulantium contraventionum"</note>. To what extent these promises may be relied upon, the reader can judge by comparing the preceding two chapters and the vanguard of the Roman-Spanish Chancellery and its daily acts themselves.

->Proverbs 26:24<-

> An enemy is known by his speech, even though he is false in his heart. When he makes his voice gracious, believe him not, for there are seven abominations in his heart.

->Extract,<-

**The Assurance of the Three Spiritual**
**Electors regarding the Ecclesiastical Properties in the Upper and Lower**
**Saxon Circles: as occurred at the meeting of the Electors and Princes at Mühlhausen, the 10th of October [Old Style] or 20th [New Style] 1620.**

For which high and great work, however, a unanimous agreement is required—if not of all, then at least of the **obedient Estates** <term>Gehorsamen Ständ</term> <note>The "Obedient Estates" refers to the territories and princes who remained loyal to the Catholic Emperor, as opposed to the "rebellious" Protestants.</note> of the Roman Empire,

<margin>To see who the "obedient Estates" are, look back at the two memorials in Chapter 2, numbers 4 and 5.</margin>

so that such a thing might be carried out with fame and honor, and the goal reached,

<margin>Namely, the **extermination of heretics** <note>original: "Außrottung der Ketzer"</note>—as the disobedient—and the expansion of the Catholic religion, and under this pretext, the Roman-Spanish Monarchy.</margin>

which has been set as the goal everywhere.

But because, on account of all sorts of [discord] that has broken out among the Estates—

...any deep-seated mistrust, which is difficult to overcome unless the same is—if not entirely—at least somewhat set aside and stilled,
<gloss>That is, for a short period of time, until it is no longer necessary for the Catholics.</gloss>
especially, however, according to the indications of the **Elector of Saxony's** <term>Churfürst von Sachsen</term> <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony, was the leading Lutheran prince of the era, whose support the Catholic Emperor desperately needed to maintain order.</note> Love and Electoral Grace, to remove the suspicion and anxious thoughts of the **Upper and Lower Saxon Circles** <term>Ober- vnd niedersächsischen Craiß</term> <note>The "Circles" were administrative districts of the Holy Roman Empire; these two circles encompassed most of northern and central Germany's Protestant territories.</note>, as if one would—on account of the **Bishoprics and Foundations** <term>Erz- vnd Stiffter</term> they hold, along with the associated goods—overpower them with force, violate them, and **dispossess them in practice** <note>original: "de facto entsetzen"</note>: which could be achieved through no better means than through adequate assurance:
<gloss>Of course, the Catholic side cannot conveniently strip them of the Foundations again, except through a presumed assurance: according to the proverb: "well trusted, the horse rides away." **Remarkable equivocation and most lying sincerity** <note>original: "Insignis æquivocatio & mendacissima Sinceratio"</note>: for once, the Ecclesiastical Reservation is not intended to leave the church properties behind.</gloss>
and We, the aforementioned, having weighed all this, and being ourselves inclined to favor each and every one of our fellow estates, and to maintain them in their due devotion to **His Imperial Majesty** <note>The Habsburg Emperor, Ferdinand II.</note>, as far as it lies within Us.
<gloss>Now, however, it is not within the power of the Bishops, nor even the Emperor himself, but rather the Pope alone, to dispose in any way of matters concerning religion and ecclesiastical properties: is that not a grand assurance?</gloss>
So have We, for the sake of the friendly and most humble honor and favor of the Elector of Saxony's Love and Electoral Grace: (yet with the express **reservation of the highly-grieved Peace of Religion and the Ecclesiastical Reservation incorporated therein**) <term>Geistlicher Vorbehalt</term> <note>The "Ecclesiastical Reservation" was a disputed clause in the 1555 Peace of Augsburg stating that if a Catholic prelate converted to Protestantism, he had to forfeit his lands and office. This was a primary cause of legal conflict leading to the Thirty Years' War.</note>
<gloss>This is also cited in Chapter 2, in the Memorial number 4: from which the clergy never intends to deviate, and which at its core is nothing other than an open perforation of the highly-solemnized Peace of Religion.</gloss>
through the following **Assurance** <term>Assecuration</term>, wish to assure the mentioned estates of the Upper and Lower Saxon Circles, adherents to the **Augsburg Confession** <term>Augspurgischen Confession</term> <note>The primary statement of faith for the Lutheran Church, presented to the Emperor in 1530.</note>, and thereby remove all suspicion.

...and all other Catholic Estates / such secular holders of the Archbishoprics and Bishoprics / along with the associated properties / herewith and in the best form and measure it should and can happen: <term>equivocation</term> <note>original: "æquivocatio." The commentator is warning that the language used here is intentionally ambiguous or deceptive.</note>
Provided they—

<gloss>"Provided they" is a hook. And the fulfillment of this condition will, today or tomorrow, rest in no one else’s judgment but that of the Catholics—namely, once they have finished dealing with the other Estates.</gloss>

—hold faithfully with His Imperial Majesty, both during the current **Bohemian unrest** <note>A reference to the Bohemian Revolt (1618–1620), the opening conflict of the Thirty Years' War.</note> as well as in future cases that might run contrary to the **Imperial Constitutions** <term>Reichs Constitutionen</term> <note>The fundamental laws and peace treaties of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> / and provide due assistance against all adversaries of the same / and do, advise, or undertake nothing contrary against them either **directly** <note>original: "directè"</note> or **indirectly** <note>original: "obliquè"</note> / and neither take nor withdraw any Archbishopric / Bishopric / Monastery / or ecclesiastical property from the Catholics / whether by force or other means / but rather leave them in peaceful possession of the same / and of whatever legal rights they still hold in the aforementioned Archbishoprics and Bishoprics held in secular hands: and shall also protect and help them against anyone / so that the same, now and in the future / shall not be attacked in deed, insulted, invaded, or violated / neither by the Catholics themselves / nor by others at their procurement / nor shall they be **in fact** <note>original: "de facto"</note>—outside of legal process—displaced or expelled from them:

<gloss>**And yet** <note>original: "Atqui"</note>, in this case, no one but the Catholics can be right, according to their own opinion.</gloss>

they also intend / when the aforementioned holders apply to His Imperial Majesty for **Imperial favors** <term>Indulta</term> for the administration of the **sovereign rights** <term>Regalia</term> <note>The "Regalia" were the high privileges of a prince, such as minting coins or collecting tolls, which required the Emperor's formal confirmation.</note> as well as **letters of protection** <term>protectoria</term> / being defense and protection documents against force / and according to the content of this our declaration: to neither hinder nor delay them in this. However, We wish through this **assurance** <term>Assecuration</term> and any resulting Imperial favors or protection letters, on account of the vote—

<gloss>They thus intend to keep the **majority** <term>Majora</term> <note>A reference to the majority vote in the Imperial Diet; if the Catholics maintained a majority, they could legally outvote the Protestant minority on religious matters.</note> one way or another / which is exactly the right means to maintain everything on their side **whether by right or wrong** <note>original: "sive fas sive nefas"</note> / and to execute and push their way through under the appearance of law.</gloss>

—and session, at Imperial Visitations, Deputations, and other [proceedings]...

such common and particular gatherings / granted them no more / than they have had up to now / and conceded / for constant and inviolable observance / <note>original: "ut moris est"</note> as is the custom / in all of this / We both Prince-Electors, etc.

NB. This assurance is much like the old proverb: "Wash my fur but don’t get me wet" <note>A German idiom (Wasch mir den Pelz und mach mich nicht nass) meaning to want the benefits of a situation without any of the risks or costs.</note>: what the Romanists <note>A common 17th-century Protestant term for Catholics loyal to the Pope.</note> give here with one hand / they take back with the other. They promise to uphold the Religious and Secular Peace <note>Referring to the Peace of Augsburg (1555), which attempted to regulate the coexistence of Catholicism and Lutheranism.</note> for the Estates of Lower Saxony / and to take no action against them regarding the Church properties **by force** <note>original: "de facto"</note> / and yet they exclude the **Ecclesiastical Reservation** <note>original: "Geistlichen vorbehalt." A highly controversial clause in the Peace of Augsburg stating that if a high-ranking Catholic cleric converted to Protestantism, he had to forfeit his lands and offices. Protestants generally did not recognize its validity.</note> from this promise: by virtue of which they are bound to the Roman Church / to give nothing away / or leave anything behind / but rather to bring back everything that was ever taken from it / and by whatever means and ways possible / to establish the Pope in Rome’s spiritual **jurisdiction** over the entire Empire, following the **oath** <note>original: "juraments"</note> the Emperor took at his coronation. How then can these two contradictory things stand together? The answer is this: because the Catholic side must not act **by force** <note>original: "de facto"</note> / and yet they must have the properties back (for in this case "no mouse nibbles a thread off it" <note>An idiom meaning "it is a fixed fact" or "there is no changing it."</note>: <note>original: "oportet habere"</note> it is necessary to have them) so they let the aforementioned "impartial law" take its course over them / and take them back by verdict and law. If the Protestant side then refuses to obey / it must be labeled **contempt** <note>original: "contumacia"</note>, **rebellion** / and disobedience / and legal **enforcement** <note>original: "Execution"</note> and a "matter of defense" (as they call it) must be undertaken against those who block justice. And this is then called acting not **by force** <note>original: "de facto"</note> but **by law** <note>original: "de jure"</note>: <note>original: "quia Papa & Imperator præsumuntur habere omnia jura in scriniis pectorum suorum"</note> because the Pope and the Emperor are presumed to hold all laws in the shrines of their hearts <note>A legal maxim suggesting the sovereign's will is the source of all law.</note>.

Most Kind Lord Brother and Son.

That in these my final years, amidst so many various endured persecutions, which must be understood *actively* and *passively* <note>original: "activè vnd passivè" — meaning both in the actions he took and the suffering he endured</note>: care, toil, and labor for the common good,

Collegio" — the elite group of princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor</note>,

<note>It is a great sorrow to him that there should still be an "altered," distrustful, truly German Protestant Elector in the Electoral College; and it is well believed that the Catholic side would dearly like to see the said College better unified—that is, that they were either all Roman Catholics within it, or at least that the heretics, such as the Palatinate <note>A reference to the Elector Palatine, the leading Protestant rebel at the start of the Thirty Years' War</note> (upon whose removal they plot day and night), were out of it.</note>

<note>Defiance to all Papists! and to remain steadfast, and to allow so little to leave your hearts,

->*He who caresses me more than he used to, has either deceived me or would like to deceive me.*<-

### Extract from
### a sermon given by Father Sabino of Venice / a preacher of the Capuchin Order at the Imperial Court / on November 8, 1620, in Vienna, regarding the Victory at Prague. <note>The "Victory at Prague" refers to the Battle of White Mountain, where Catholic Imperial forces defeated the Protestant Bohemian rebels.</note>

Shortly after, toward the end <note>original: "Paulò post sub fin."</note>: It is indeed true; we have won his kingdom. Namely, that they should be **dashed to pieces like vessels** <note>A biblical reference to Psalm 2:9, often used in this era to justify the harsh suppression of rebels or "heretics"</note>, and that those things which are unsightly and abominable to God the Lord should be put out of sight and cleared away; their privileges and freedoms should be taken from them, their concessions torn to pieces, and the **Letter of Majesty** <term>Letter of Majesty <note>A 1609 charter granted by Emperor Rudolf II that guaranteed religious freedom to the Bohemian estates; its revocation and physical destruction by Emperor Ferdinand II was a pivotal moment in the suppression of Protestantism.</note></term> torn up and poked full of holes, and thus, in short, to make such subjects of them as are entirely and solely submissive to the Crown in all things.

#### Of the Lords and Estates in Moravia <note>A historical region in the modern-day Czech Republic that was a major center of the Protestant uprising</note>, whose estates have been restored: this is a clear mirror of how religion is abolished by every way and means.
