<meta>This page continues the diplomatic correspondence from the Elector of Saxony to the Elector of Mainz, dated February 23, 1623. It continues a legal argument regarding the controversial transfer of the Electoral title from the Elector Palatine to the Duke of Bavaria during the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

...could easily arise, so that the Electors can be assured against the Empire. It also cannot be excused by appealing to necessity, or by claiming that it should be without prejudice to the **Electoral College** <note>The elite group of princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor</note>; for the **Electoral Capitulations** <note>The constitutional contracts sworn by an Emperor upon his election; original: *Capitulationes*</note> are a matter of <term>strict law</term> <note>original: *stricti juris*; meaning they must be followed exactly as written without flexible interpretation</note> and admit no exceptions. The limitations and interpretations of these rest with the entire Electoral College, and the protests and declarations already made are of little effect when the <term>actual deed is to the contrary</term> <note>original: *factum in contrarium*</note>.

For this and many other reasons, we have found it necessary to indicate our position in the votes brought forward, doubting not that this will be taken not poorly, but rather as something useful for the preservation of the Electors' high status. We have considered the <term>transfer</term> <note>original: *translatio*</note> of the Electoral title and what follows from it not as a means of peace, but rather as a cause for everlasting war, based on many cited motives. Therefore, we have been unable to approve it and remain of that mind once more—especially since not a single example exists since the establishment of the **Golden Bull** <note>The 1356 decree settling the Imperial election process</note> where such a thing has occurred. Not only is there no precedent, but the case of Elector Maurice <note>Maurice of Saxony (1521–1553), who was granted the Saxon Electorate after it was stripped from his cousin, proving a complex precedent for title transfers</note> testifies to the opposite. 

We do not know whether the clause found in the Resolution of His **Roman Imperial Majesty** issued to the Electors and Princes—concerning children and **agnates** <note>Male-line relatives who had a legal claim to inheritance</note>—will cause further delay and bitterness rather than peace. This is because, strictly speaking, <term>through the crime of another</term> <note>original: *per delictum alienum*</note>, even a third and innocent party could be deprived of their <term>right acquired through their own merit</term> <note>original: *jus per proprium factum quæsitum*</note> and thus be stripped of their <term>joint inheritance</term> <note>original: *gesampten Handt*; a legal term where multiple family members hold a right together</note>. It has therefore been recognized that children and relatives are being directed toward a "gracious settlement" or "legal judgment," which turns what was certain into something uncertain and subject to dispute. This concerns and affects not only the Palatine families but all Electors and Princes who have attained their status by <term>their own act</term> <note>original: *proprio facto*</note> through collective tenure, and who have at all times followed this by performing the oath and "kissing the sword" <note>A traditional ceremony of feudal loyalty</note>. It is therefore unacceptable to prejudice the entire Empire through the approval and whim of a single party. Without seeking personal glory, we mention that we have [rendered] to His **Roman Imperial Majesty**, our most Gracious Lord, such...

->Extract of the Records:<-
### What has transpired against the Imperial Cities since the Accord of Aschaffenburg.

After the three convening, formerly united cities—namely Strasbourg, Nuremberg, and Ulm—recently observed that the Elector of Saxony and Burggrave of Magdeburg, etc., our most gracious Lord, took little pleasure in the <term>Union</term> <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of German Protestant states formed in 1608</note> at that time; indeed, His Electoral Grace even directed and applied his own powerful military forces to the service of the Roman Imperial Majesty, our most gracious Lord. This exemplary precedent by the most powerful head of the Lutheran religion in the Holy Roman Empire strongly moved the united Imperial Cities to <term>abandon</term> <note>original: *deseriren*</note> the aforementioned Protestant Alliance and to withdraw their hands from the defense of the Electoral Palatinate. They were moved to comply with and assist the Imperial Majesty, along with the Electors and Princes who were gathered at Mühlhausen, in their most gracious, gracious, and favorable <term>demands</term> <note>original: *postulatis*</note>. This was all the more so because the Roman Imperial Majesty further assured the said united Imperial Cities under the Imperial hand and seal that His Imperial Majesty would <term>enact a perpetual amnesty</term> <note>original: *amnistiam sanciren*; a legal decree of pardon and oblivion for past political offenses</note>, the

cities were to be burdened with no garrison, nor with any troop marches through them; likewise, that the cities—whether from His Imperial Majesty, the King of Spain, any Archduke of Austria, or any other Catholic Estate—should have to fear no violence, pressure, or loss, whether secretly or openly, directly or indirectly. His Electoral Grace of Saxony mentioned this Aschaffenburg settlement and that one should rely upon it on the 15th of December, 1621, and the 19th of January, 1622. Archduke Albert—as the eldest Arch-Prince of the most illustrious House of Austria—did so on the 14th of April, 1621. <meta>This page continues the argument from page 65, emphasizing that the Imperial Cities acted in good faith based on the security guarantees provided by the Emperor and other high Catholic authorities. It describes the significant sacrifices the cities made—such as leaving the Protestant Union—to maintain their neutrality and peace.</meta>

...already recounted—indeed, even more strong **promises** <note>original: *promissa*</note>, **assurances** <note>original: *assecurationes*</note>, and **pledges of sincerity** <note>original: *Sincerationes*</note> could be documented and presented in the **original** <note>original: *originaliter*</note>, if necessary.

Upon such high, often-repeated Imperial, Royal, Electoral, Archducal, Princely, and Comital words—which carry the **weight of an oath** <note>original: *vim juramenti*</note>—the <term>Imperial Cities</term> <note>original: *Reichsstätt*; cities that were self-governing and subordinate only to the Holy Roman Emperor</note> have until now relied so firmly, with the deepest observance and respect, that they even **withdrew** <note>original: *recedirt*</note> and departed from the **Union** <note>the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant states formed for defense, which dissolved around 1621</note> they previously held. They dismissed the strong **army** <note>original: *armee*</note> maintained together with other high Estates; they notably reduced their own garrisons and city defenses to remove any mistrust; they permitted, allowed, and granted the Imperial <note>indicated by the abbreviation "K." for *Kaiserlich*</note> Generals and Colonels the enlistment of their discharged city troops; they patiently bore all the ingratitude incurred from the warring <term>Palatine party</term> <note>original: *Pfältzischen Parthey*; the supporters of Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King" whose election to the Bohemian throne sparked the Thirty Years' War</note> and its **adherents** <note>original: *Adhærenten*</note>, as well as the unspeakable damage caused by unheard-of demands, inhumane arson, robbery, pillaging, and the murder of many people. They held solely and exclusively to the <term>Imperial Constitutions</term> <note>original: *constitutiones Imperij*; the fundamental laws governing the Holy Roman Empire</note> and the cited Accord of Aschaffenburg, maintained themselves in true **neutrality** <note>original: *Neutralitet*</note>, and **in short** <note>original: *in summa*</note> **governed** <note>original: *gubernirt*</note> themselves in such a way that they constantly trusted they could answer for it both before God and before all dispassionate, honest hearts and minds.

It did indeed cut the Cities deeply to the heart and soul that immediately after the conclusion of the Recess of Aschaffenburg, they had to hear bitter insults, harsh threats, and even reports of hostile **counsels** <note>original: *consilijs*</note> and plots, feeling that they received very poor thanks for their loyalty and **devotion** <note>original: *devotion*</note>. This is just as **experience** <note>original: *experientz*</note> makes known; and one would have to recount for several days if one wished merely to **review** <note>original: *recensiren*</note> and tell of the most significant, intentional grievances that the Cities actually encountered in various places at the hands of the Spanish, Bavarian, and Archduke Leopold’s soldiery. Yet one swallowed it all nonetheless, let the insult pass patiently, and also wished

...[just-recounted]; indeed even several more strong **promises** <note>original: *promissa*</note>, **assurances** <note>original: *assecurationes*</note>, and **guarantees of indemnity** <note>original: *Sincerationes*</note> for cases of emergency can be documented and presented in the **original** <note>original: *originaliter*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the account of Archduke Leopold’s campaign in the Upper Rhine. It did not end here; rather, His Princely Highness Archduke Leopold of Austria (according to the personal and far-reaching report of the Lord Bishop of Speyer) intended to enter Speyer with his entire military armada—including the wild <term>Poles and Cossacks</term> <note>Likely the *Lisowczycy*, a notorious Polish-Lithuanian mercenary light cavalry unit known for their speed and brutality</note>—as an <term>Imperial Commissioner</term> <note>original: *K. Commissarij*; a representative acting with the Emperor's legal authority</note>, and to take the city into actual Imperial protection and defense (as they now choose to call and color it). However, due to high-level intercession, this was somewhat mitigated; yet the Archduke’s side handled matters in such a way that it did not pass without incredible terror, and everyone was in great doubt as to whether the Archduke’s arrival was to be seen as a sign of favor or disfavor.

Upon the aforementioned city of Speyer, the highly esteemed Archduke Leopold imposed a strong and—given the circumstances of the poor citizens of Speyer—unaffordable garrison. When the city referred to its ancient <term>privileges</term> <note>original: *Privilegia*; legal rights or exemptions granted to a city or group</note>, which had been very recently confirmed by His Imperial Majesty and specifically served as a protection against such a garrison, and then cited the <term>Aschaffenburg Recess</term> <note>An agreement from 1620 between the Emperor and the Protestant Union regarding the neutrality of certain regions</note>, which had been corroborated by His Imperial Majesty and many Electors, Archdukes, Princes, and Lords, the council was answered by the Archduke’s and the Bishop’s representatives: The privileges had their place, and no harm was being done to them by the Imperial decree; but the Aschaffenburg agreement was made according to the times and circumstances; since those times had undergone change and alteration, the situation was now quite different. Furthermore, the Roman Emperors, in granting favors, freedoms, and privileges, had not tied their own hands for future cases that might arise. Other proceedings (namely the Aschaffenburg agreement), being measured by the time, were also subject to the changes and alterations of the era, and His Imperial Majesty’s plans could not be obstructed; indeed, the Archduke’s councilors were hesitant even to speak to His Princely Highness about it or to raise any objections.

Thereupon, the <term>garrison</term> <note>original: *Guarnison*; a body of troops stationed in a fortified place</note> of the city of Speyer...

<meta>This page continues the account of the occupation of Speyer by the forces of Archduke Leopold. The previous page ended with the city Council attempting to cite its imperial privileges to avoid a garrison.</meta>

...more was added to the burden. The citizens were disarmed, and the Council was given assurances that once the citizens were disarmed, the garrison would be reduced, and that the disarmament would also apply to both the clergy and the <term>Chamber officials</term> <note>original: *Camerales*; officials and staff of the Imperial Chamber Court (*Reichskammergericht*), which was based in Speyer</note>.

However, after the disarmament was completed, the garrison was not reduced at all. The Chamber officials were not disarmed either; their <term>privileges</term> were cited as a reason, with the claim that their immunities could not be breached. Yet, on the other hand, the good city of Speyer was allowed nothing of its own privileges, freedoms, and treaties. Furthermore, the city of Speyer was accused of being part of the <term>Union</term> <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states that opposed the Catholic League</note>. They were even pressured to heartlessly cast out all the innocent people who had fled their homes in the countryside and sought refuge in the city. For this reason, searches were conducted from house to house; locksmiths and coopers were used to force open all chests and barrels, demanding the surrender of all goods brought into the city by those fleeing the countryside. 

The houses of wealthy people were plundered, the citizens were squeezed beyond their means, and <term>distinguished persons</term> <note>original: *honoratas personas*</note> were literally scared to death by the terror and cruelty inflicted upon them. The city was threatened with a general looting; many thousands of guilders were extorted from the Council itself. All the soldiers hired by the city were dismissed, the regular city watches were abolished and replaced with foreign soldiers. In short, such actions were taken that hardly any angry <term>sovereign</term> <note>original: *Landesfürst*</note>, moved to the highest level of displeasure, would practice more harshly against his own hereditary subjects.

As for what the current news reports may further bring: namely, that even the personnel of the <term>Imperial Chamber Court</term> (who, <note>original: *vigore ordinationis cameræ*</note> by virtue of the court’s ordinance, are under the special protection and shield of the Holy Empire) were not entirely spared. Instead of <term>safeguards</term> <note>original: *SalvaGuardien*; official guards or signs posted on a building to indicate it was under military protection</note>, gallows were painted on the houses of the Lutheran city pastors. While they stood in their pulpits, they were shamefully mocked, ridiculed, and despised during the public sermons; they were even shot at. There may be many more such disorderly dealings, for the sufficient telling of which <note>original: *non [horæ]*; from the catchword *horæ*, meaning "hours [are not enough]"</note>

<summary>This page describes the betrayal and brutal treatment of the city of Speyer by Imperial forces, including the disarmament of citizens, the extortion of funds, and the targeted harassment of Lutheran clergy.</summary>

...hours, not days, but whole weeks <note>original: *horæ, non dies, sed integræ septimanæ*</note> would be required—is left as it is until the honorable, oppressed people have more breathing room, better security, and no longer have cause to fear further distress. In the good city of Speyer, this process has not yet concluded or ended. Instead, His Princely Grace <note>original: *F. D.* - *Fürstliche Durchlaucht*</note>, Archduke Leopold, acting as an Imperial Commissioner, also traveled to Worms. The Council of Worms could not prevent this entry without provoking great displeasure, showing disrespect to the Roman Emperor, and jeopardizing the welfare of all its citizens; thus, they had to allow it to happen.

The keys to the city gates were also demanded from the aforementioned city of Worms. However, in the end—as with the other cities—the <term>soldiery</term> <note>original: *Soldatesca*</note> shared control with the <term>magistrate</term> <note>The city's governing body of civil officials</note>. For their maintenance, the city had to provide 16,000 <term>florins</term> <note>original: *fl.*; a standard currency of the Holy Roman Empire</note> monthly. Despite this, the city remained in constant, visible danger of further burdens, deception, and harassment—and indeed, they had to live in constant fear of being plundered.

Neither in Speyer nor in Worms did His Princely Grace allow the famous Imperial Commission to be seen, neither in copy nor in its original form <note>original: *in copia noch originaliter*</note>. His Princely Grace also claimed that His Imperial Majesty had assigned a collective commission against the city of Worms to the Lord Elector of Mainz, to His Princely Grace himself, and to the Lord Bishop of Speyer—thus to three Commissioners. Yet, no sub-delegates from Mainz or from the Bishop of Speyer appeared, and furthermore, no one at the court of the Elector of Mainz seemed to know anything about such a commission.

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Archduke Leopold’s arrival in Worms. It details the legal and political maneuvers used by the Imperial Commission to bypass previous treaties and justify the continued military occupation and financial burden placed upon the city, following the pattern established in Speyer.</meta>

Regarding the Aschaffenburg **agreement**, which had been so highly and solemnly confirmed, the Archducal and Leopoldian **sub-delegates** in the city of Worms argued that while the said treaty had indeed been established "as things stood then" <note>original: *rebus ut tunc stantibus*</note>, the times had since changed <note>original: *alterirt*</note>. They claimed the danger was now even greater, even though the mercenary commander **Mansfeld** had actually retreated forty or fifty miles away from there. 

They argued that this matter—the installation of **garrisons**—was a special and more recent Imperial Commission. They further remarked that for the Council of Worms to so diligently cite <note>original: *allegire*</note> the Aschaffenburg <term>Recess</term> <note>The official summary of decisions made at a diplomatic meeting; in this case, a treaty signed in Aschaffenburg that was supposed to protect the cities</note> was merely a "German custom" practiced "so that one might demonstrate one's own diligence" <note>original: *ut quis docere possit de sua diligentia*</note>. If the city insisted on adhering <note>original: *inhæriren*</note> to the agreement in that way, His Princely Highness <note>original: *J. F. D.* - *Ihre Fürstliche Durchlaucht*, referring to Archduke Leopold</note> was determined not to depart from Worms. Instead, he intended to report this to His **Imperial Majesty** via his own dispatched courier and await His Majesty’s most gracious resolution while remaining there with his horsemen and soldiers at the city’s expense.

Finally, when it was requested and urgently petitioned <note>original: *sollicitirt*</note> that the surrender terms <note>original: *Capitulation*</note> should at least mention the Aschaffenburg agreement and ensure it remained unchanged in other points, the Archduke’s representatives refused to hear any more of it. Instead, they let their position be known with these words: that they would let the agreement "rest on its own merit and lack of merit."

In the beginning, to somewhat sweeten and "sugar-coat" the sour burden of the heavy **garrisons**, both cities, Speyer and Worms, were given hope that such an occupation would only last two months, until the approaching **assembly** <note>original: *conventu*</note> of Electors and Princes. It was suggested that His Princely Highness would take some of the troops with him to the **Imperial Diet at Regensburg**. Afterward, however, when a written document was requested to confirm this time limit, they cast the matter into doubt. They qualified the promise with the word "hopefully" and, in short, refused to interpret it as anything other than this: the aforementioned Archduke and the Bishop of Speyer would indeed intercede <note>original: *intercediren*</note> with the Imperial Majesty, but they would promise <note>original: *promittiren*</note> nothing binding regarding the outcome <note>original: *de eventü*</note>. Instead, it remained entirely in their...

<summary>Archduke Leopold's representatives use legal loopholes and "sugar-coated" promises to ignore the Aschaffenburg treaty and maintain a military occupation in Worms and Speyer. The city is forced to house and fund troops under the threat of Imperial displeasure despite the retreat of enemy forces.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the report on the military occupation of the Imperial Cities, specifically detailing the plight of Speyer and Worms despite their proven loyalty, and introduces a formal protest from the Electors of Saxony and Brandenburg regarding the redistribution of the Palatine titles.</meta>

Majesty <note>original: *Mtt:* — His Imperial Majesty, Ferdinand II</note> would provide a future resolution. All of this has happened to the 
cities of Speyer and Worms without any cause given. Even the Archducal Councilors and Commissaries themselves have acknowledged 
and admitted that neither of the two cities gave any cause for displeasure, but rather maintained themselves in *Imperial devotion* <note>original: *K. devotion* — loyalty to the Emperor</note>. This was even testified to by the clergy, the monks, and the Jesuits themselves; yet, they were treated in the aforementioned cruel manner—no differently or less severely than if they had been the most disobedient of all—and no letters or treaties they cited <note>original: *allegiren*</note> were respected.

The Bavarian General, Count Tilly, has also kept a garrison in the city of Wimpfen for many months. It is not difficult to imagine what unbearable burdens such a distressed city has endured under an unrestrained occupation, heavy troop movements, frequent retreats within its walls, and the great field battles fought in that vicinity <note>original: *in illa vicinia*</note>.

Thus, nine Evangelical <term>Imperial Cities</term> <note>Cities that were self-governing and subordinate only to the Emperor, rather than a local prince</note> have now been taken by the Spanish, Archducal Leopoldian, and Bavarian troops; namely: Worms, Speyer, Haguenau, Wissembourg, Landau, Wimpfen, Friedberg, and Gelnhausen. What will happen to the rest, who are threatened daily and hear of strange developments, is known only to the dear God, who wishes to comfort all those in distress.

->Num. 14.<-

...issued / We also had our envoys, both within and outside the councils, vote, remind, and indicate the means and ways so that we might bring back the long-desired peace and quiet and lift and settle all the rebellion that has arisen / since it is sufficiently and abundantly known to Your Imperial Majesty <note>original: *E. R. M.* — *Euer Römischen Kaiserlichen Majestät*</note>, and could be understood from the **votes** <note>original: *votis*</note> and **objections** <note>original: *contradictionibus*</note> presented to the same, we for our part only wish that such reminders and faithfully declared **votes** and **objections** had been well received and addressed in a way that was necessary and possible for the sought-after peace / for we have never sought anything else here than constant rest, peace, and good intentions between the Head and the members <note>A common legal metaphor where the Emperor is the "Head" and the Imperial Estates are the "Members" of the body politic of the Empire</note>; and it is hoped that the expected **outcome** <note>original: *eventus*</note> will testify to this more fully / and since such faithful reminders and declared **votes** did not flow from **private motives** <note>original: *privat affecten*</note> or a desire to cause unnecessary expansion of the conflict, but rather from the **Fundamental Laws, Imperial Constitutions, Imperial precedents, Nature, and Equity** / and it does not lie within our power to **change** <note>original: *zumutiren*</note>, to **declare**, to abolish, or to **nullify** <note>original: *zucassiren*</note> them at our pleasure, but rather to hold firmly to them by virtue of the solemn duty with which we are bound to Your Imperial Majesty and the Holy Roman Empire / so we do not see how we could withdraw from this or declare ourselves differently to Your Imperial Majesty than has already been done through our envoys; <meta>This page continues the formal address from the Protestant Electors to the Emperor. ...rial Majesty, and have caused other <term>Electors</term> <note>German: *Churfürsten*; the high-ranking princes who held the right to elect the Emperor</note> and Princes unnecessary trouble and expense, or that our envoys dispatched there did not have in their instructions <note>original: *in mandatis*</note> what they brought forward and presented. This would be not only blameworthy and shameful, but also disparaging among other high and great <term>Potentates</term> <note>Powerful rulers or monarchs</note> within and outside the Empire, especially those who shared our opinion. We also would not know how we could answer for it to our posterity or to other Princes and <term>Estates</term> <note>The constituent territories and rulers represented in the Imperial Diet</note> of the Empire.

For although we do not deny, but rather gladly admit, that an Elector or Prince, just like anyone else, should be punished for his crimes—of which there are sufficient examples <note>original: *Exempla*</note> within the Holy Roman Empire—nevertheless, Your Imperial Majesty will graciously agree with us that an Elector or Prince, in matters of punishment, cannot be in a **worse condition** <note>original: *deterioris conditionis*</note> than common people, against whom **one never proceeds without a hearing or a summons** <note>original: *vngehöret vnd vncitiret*</note>, even if the crimes are **notorious** <note>original: *crimina notoria*; crimes so obvious they require no further proof</note>. In this, Your Imperial Majesty’s rightful <term>jurisdiction</term> <note>Legal authority</note> is not called into doubt in any way, but is rather acknowledged, provided that the **manner of proceeding** <note>original: *modus procedendi*</note> is undertaken in such a way as the **Fundamental Laws and Imperial Ordinances** require.

Your Imperial Majesty is well aware that, even according to common feudal laws, when a dispute arises between a liege lord and a vassal, it must be decided and discussed by **peers of the court** <note>original: *per pares curiae*</note>, those of equal status <note>literally "equal shield and name"</note>. How much more, then, in the punishment of higher and more significant persons, should those be consulted and asked, according to the terms of the <term>Capitulation</term> <note>The *Wahlkapitulation*, or electoral agreement, which the Emperor swore to uphold upon his election</note>: specifically those who are Your Imperial Majesty’s innermost and most secret councilors, and who are equal in rank, honor, and dignity to the person who is to be punished.

What our sentiment and opinion were regarding the **Transfer** <note>original: *Translation*; referring here to the controversial transfer of the Palatine electoral dignity to the Duke of Bavaria</note> of the Electorate is likewise not unknown to Your Imperial Majesty; we have also had it extensively explained how this was handled in the past; and we must also

<summary>The Electors argue that even high-ranking princes are entitled to due process and a trial by peers under Imperial law, protesting against summary punishments.
